Inasmuch as we recognize the fundamental significance of the sense of touch for the development of our life of sensation, we have also grasped the imagination of touch as the precondition for the development of our whole life of fantasy. Whoever therefore wishes to become acquainted with the nature of human imagination must in a scientific investigation take the imagination of the man born blind as his point of departure, because only the man born blind possesses an autonomous fantasy of touch in the sense characterized above. The consideration of this independent fantasy of touch now shows that a man born blind is able to represent to himself an arbitrary figure, for example a triangle, only by producing the figure in question through imagined movements of the hand. If he runs his hand over the four edges of a tabletop, he is capable of grasping the top as quadrangular only because he is afterward able to produce the quadrangular form in his fantasy through imagined movement of the hand. Were he not capable of letting the hand glide over the surfaces and edges of a body in reality and also in imagination, then he would also not be in a position to perceive the shape of the body in question. All perception of extension, position, and shape of bodies is bound to the execution of real and imagined movements about their surfaces and contours. This proposition holds in the same compass for the seeing man as for the blind, only its validity is harder to establish for the seeing man, because in him the fantasy of touch is covered over by the fantasy of sight. When we who see lead our gaze round along the boundary line of a hoop, then our hand performs this movement along with it in imagination, only the imagined accompanying movement of the hand usually scarcely comes to our consciousness on account of its slight liveliness. Moreover, we are able to perceive the shape of things by means of the sense of sight too only because we can let the gaze glide along the outlines of bodies in reality as well as in imagination. Without real and imagined gliding of the gaze, as also without real and imagined movements of the limbs, there is for us no perceivable grasping of any spatial shapes. But even the mere grasping of a place in space, the mere transposing of a thing or a process to some spatial site, would be impossible if we were not in a position to point to the place in question through real and imagined movements. When the surface of our skin is touched at some point by a foreign body, we know, even if we do not see, how to state fairly approximately—indeed for certain particularly sensitive places with greater exactitude—where the contact took place; we "localize," as one is wont to express it, with greater or lesser sharpness our sensations of skin contact at the outer surface of our body and no less at certain mucous membranes of its inner surface. In general we shall know how to localize most sharply the contacts at the movable parts of our body and then also in those regions toward which we are wont often and with ease to execute movements in reality and in imagination. For what does it mean to know the place at which our body is touched? It means as much as to be capable of making in imagination a pointing movement toward that place. The localizing movement carried out in imagination we can, provided only that we are not prevented from it by any paralysis, translate into a real movement, that is, through an actually executed movement of the hand ourselves touch approximately that place of the skin where the contact by the foreign body took place. The capacity to perform movements in imagination, or, as one may also say, virtual movements, forms the foundation of our whole life of fantasy. We therefore owe it to our fantasy, more exactly speaking to our imagined or virtual movements, that we are able to localize contacts at the surface of the skin, and that we are capable of assigning to things or processes some place in space. When we say that we perceive a body at a quite determinate place of space, then there is thereby meant in the first instance that we execute in imagination a movement toward that place at which the body is found. It is virtual pointings through which we fix the places of things and appearances in space, and through virtual movements along the surfaces and outlines of bodies we grasp their extension, position, and shape. And so the sense of the fundamental proposition of the theory of perception now becomes clear to us: that without fantasy, and namely without virtual movement, there can nowhere be a perception of spatially ordered, disposed, and shaped things or appearances. The perception of the real world would remain closed to us if we possessed no imagination, for imagination, and namely imagined movement, is unconditionally requisite in order that anything spatial may be perceived. Modern psychology does not know this concept of fantasy, because the concept of imagined or virtual movement, as it is here conceived, is likewise wanting to it. The English founders of modern psychology, in particular Locke, Berkeley, and Hume, are at bottom sensualists, that is, thinkers who reduce perception and finally also every activity of knowledge in general to sensations of the senses and if possible to sensations of the senses alone. Unfortunately their influence extends not merely to the proper adherents of their school but to all philosophical schools of the last two or two and a half centuries, so that their fundamental views work determiningly upon the thinking of all educated men. We all live, so to speak, in the prejudice that perceptions of the senses must be psychologically resolved into mere sensations of the senses. Now sensations are indeed the first and unconditionally requisite conditions of the activity of perception of our consciousness, yet they are by no means the sufficient conditions for it, since, as we convinced ourselves, imagination, or the virtual movement, is likewise as indispensable a condition of the activity of perception as sensation itself. That pure sensations do not suffice for the activity of perception confronts us nowhere so vividly as in the developmental history of the infant. The newborn shows sensation already in the first days of its existence, but of an activity of perception there can scarcely be talk in it for weeks, indeed for months. How does this come about? Were processes of sensation the sufficient substratum for an activity of perception, then already the infant of a few days would have to manifest an activity of perception. But of this there is not a trace present. For the infant at first lacks every kind of virtual movements. It does indeed move its little hands already in the first days of life, but it merely travels to and fro with them aimlessly, travels also into its face and into its eyes and thereby at times injures itself; in short, it lacks all steering or governance of its movements. But I say that a movement is steered when a corresponding imagined movement precedes it, that is, when it is the realization of a preceding virtual movement. Only in the measure that the infant learns to steer its movements of hand and eye, hence only in the measure that it is able through virtual movements to give its real movements a direction and form, in short in the measure that an imagination develops, does it also learn to transpose what is sensed to a place and to perceive shaped things or appearances in space. But because now the modern philosophy of sensation proceeding from England knows nothing of the fundamental significance of imagination for every activity of perception, because it does not know the concept of imagined movement, it falls everywhere into the highest embarrassment where it is a matter of explaining how we arrive at the perception of spatially ordered and shaped things and appearances. In the first place it knows how to give us no information as to how it is possible that we transpose the contact of our body to a determinate place of the surface of the skin even with bandaged eyes. To be sure, the subtle Lotze wished to explain this through his well-known hypothesis of local signs, according to which the sensations of contact would be affected with a something, with a local sign, through which the place of the sensation is made known to us: but at bottom everyone feels that this hypothesis is only a mere paraphrase of the fact that we localize our sensations of contact. What that something may be which must be enclosed in the sensation in order to assign it a place remains shrouded in mystical darkness, and the word "local sign" is only a means of deceiving us about this darkness. Most investigators understand by the local sign some kind of accompanying sensation—I had almost said "aftertaste"—which is associated with the tactile sensations and doubtless also with the visual sensations in order to indicate their locality. Now it is indeed true that the sensations of contact of the various parts of the surface of the body can show qualitative differences, but qualitative differences are precisely of a qualitative and not of a local kind, and are therefore in themselves wholly unsuited to indicate the place of a contact. The whole hypothesis of local signs is only a vivid presentation of the impossibility of getting at the problem of localization upon a sensualistic foundation. One attempts in vain to read into a tactile sensation something mysterious that could make known its place, for what is puzzled into the tactile sensation is after all in the end again only sensation: but a sensation of place there simply is not. For places are nothing of the sort that would have the power to awaken sensations in us. Sensations are excited in us only through physical-chemical processes; but since places are not such processes, no rational person will ascribe to them the capacity to be able to act upon our sensitive nerves. Physiologists do indeed since Ernst Heinrich Weber's path-breaking investigations of the senses of the skin (1846) use the expression "sense of place" for the capacity to localize the tactile sensations at the surface of the skin; but they are more or less clearly conscious that of a sense of place one cannot speak in the meaning of sense of warmth, sense of color, and so forth, because warmth and light are physical energies to which within our life-process proper modes of sensation correspond, whereas spatial places represent no energy which could claim for itself a proper class of sensations of the senses, hence a particular "sense." Critically thinking physiologists, such as for example Thunberg, therefore also admonish that the expression "sense of place," which has become traditional in the physiology of the skin senses, be avoided altogether. Because space in itself is not able to awaken any sensations in us, it is also not sensation taken by itself through which its existence is disclosed to us. But although space is nothing sensible, it is nevertheless something perceivable: an apparent paradox in which the enigmatic nature of space attains its most concise expression. For one commonly supposes that only what is sensible is at the same time also perceivable; but the existence of space is as it were an omnipresent mockery of this apparently correct proposition, for space is indeed what is everywhere perceivable and yet is nowhere able to act upon the sensing nerves. Therefore space is also, so to speak, an omnipresent veto against that one-sidedly sensualistic theory of knowledge which proceeded from England and which strove in crude fashion to reduce all perception, and hence also all abstract knowledge, to pure sensing. Only a being which not only has sensation but is also able to produce movements and—what is the chief thing—also virtual movements can possess a perception of space. It is not the sensations that make a shape known to us; sensations are there only to excite our imagination, and it is only this imagination that lets us grasp the shape. If, for example, I cover with the palm of my hand the circular opening of a beaker, I do not perceive the circular form of the opening by means of the sensation which the rim of the glass awakens, but these sensations must first excite my imagination and prompt it to an imagined movement round about the rim of the glass, in order that I may be able to grasp its circular form. Sensations are indeed an indispensable condition for the perception of any really existing shapes of things, but it is not the sensations themselves, but the virtual movements awakened by them, that disclose to us the shapes. One stands here before the wonderful fact that the real spatial order of things is made known to us through fantasy, so that without it, and namely without virtual movements, we should not have the slightest inkling of the real arrangement, disposition, and configuration of the actually subsisting world of appearance. Imagination is, so to speak, the organ for the grasping of space or of the spatial relations of the real world of appearance. To be sure, the expression "organ" is to be understood only in a figurative sense: in truth it is only meant that without the process of virtual movement no perception of spatial structures would be possible. When there is talk of imagination, one commonly thinks at once of waking reveries or of poetic-artistic activity of fantasy, so that one deprives oneself from the outset of the possibility of understanding the processes of imagination. Even Kant still defines the power of imagination in the following one-sided manner: "The power of imagination is the faculty of representing an object in intuition even without its presence"; but there are found in his "Deduction of the Pure Concepts of the Understanding" remarkable trains of thought which seek to overcome this one-sidedness; indeed, in the first edition of the "Critique of Pure Reason" there is found a most interesting note which begins with the following sentence: "That the power of imagination is a necessary ingredient of perception itself has surely occurred to no psychologist as yet." Nevertheless Kant brings it to no clarity in this direction, because he does not yet form the concept of imagined or virtual movement as the counterpart to real movement and consequently, despite all his resistance, remains caught in the sensualistic psychology of the English. In order now wholly to overcome this sensualistic influence, we shall have to make a distinction between that imagination through which our consciousness is directly chained to the facts of the real environment, and that imagination which frees us from the chains of the real environment. There is an imagination which makes our mind "absent" in relation to the real processes acting upon our sensing nerves, and commonly only this absence-making imagination is comprehended under the concept of fantasy; but there is—as we have just shown—also an imagination which is stimulated by the sensation of the present processes and to which we owe it that the present is able to unroll before us in its spatial order, disposition, and configuration. I designate this latter, which binds our consciousness to what is present and through which what is present is fashioned into a spatial environment, as the direct fantasy, and set over against it as its antagonist the inverse fantasy, through which our consciousness becomes a stranger in the real environment. This is the fundamental distinction in the doctrine of imagination, and because it is not made by modern psychology, the latter is unable to rouse itself to a theory of fantasy at all, but remains a slave of English philosophy of sensation. Our imagination is unable to fasten itself to what is present for any length of time, but leaps in intermittent pauses involuntarily away from the present things, only, to be sure, to return swiftly to them again. This leaping away from reality and returning to it, this wavering between the dominion of the inverse and of the direct fantasy, can be figuratively represented by a wave-line in which, say, the wave-crests may represent the phases of the direct and the wave-troughs the phases of the inverse fantasy. We let ourselves be taken captive by the impressions of the present, but our imagination glides quite unnoticed over to images of the future or of the past, so that for instants we are carried away from the present, only to return to it again and to dwell with it. It is a tremendously complicated play of waves of which there is talk here, for the processes of the environment stimulate the most manifold processes of sensation and thus occupy our direct imagination in an involved manner, while on the other hand surges of feeling, agitations of temperament, affects, through their continual ebb and flow, seek to divert our attention from the processes of the present in order to turn it to the images of hope of the future or the images of memory of the past, or to lead it back again to reality. Where is the psychology which would have seriously taken in hand the exploration of this boundlessly involved play of waves of our life of imagination, as it is influenced on the one side by the impressions of the environment and on the other by the inner floods of feelings and affects? English psychology has devised for the characterization of these extraordinarily complicated connections in the activity of fantasy the expression "association of ideas." A hollower word has surely never been introduced into serious philosophical investigations concerning human nature. To be sure, our experiences are associated with one another, just as the waves of the ocean, the orbits of the planets, and in general everything that ever took place is thoroughly associated with everything that ever will take place. It is only a question what is gained if instead of the expressions "connection" or "linkage" we use the Latin word "association." Through the introduction of the technical expression "association" into modern psychology it has succeeded in diverting attention precisely from those processes—namely the processes of imagination—to which we owe association, that is, the connection of our experiences of sensation and our processes of feeling. Let this be shown at once by an example. If I cover the opening of a beaker with the palm of my hand, then I perceive, as has been said, its circular form. The tactile sensations which are awakened by the individual parts of the rim of the glass do not remain isolated, but combine into a circular form. The modern psychologist says with a learned mien that they have associated themselves and through this association produced the representation of the circle of the glass rim. Yes, but what process is it then really through which the tactile sensations were associated with one another? The association psychologist finds no answer, for he seems to be of the view that the word "association" possesses the magical power of setting tactile sensations into connection with one another. Thus the word association serves him for the peculiar purpose of conjuring out of the world precisely that interesting process through which the association of the tactile sensations was brought about: I mean the "imagined movement," to which we owe it that our consciousness was able to grasp the circular shape of the rim of the glass. Another example. When one contemplates the features of a person A, it can easily happen that one is reminded of the features of a person B resembling him. The psychologist speaks here of an association through similarity. Thereby, however, the fact escapes him which upon this occasion ought in the first rank to awaken his interest. For when we contemplate the person A, our direct imagination is as it were laid in fetters by his facial features; but suddenly these fetters loosen, and our mind leaps away from the real figure which stands before us in order to dwell for an instant upon the features of another person whom we perhaps saw in a foreign land and many years ago. The wonderful thing in this happening is surely first of all that there is in us a power which is able to burst the bonds that chain us to the present through our sensation and direct imagination! Who names this power which with magic fingers looses us from the ensnarement of present impressions and makes it possible for us to dwell for an instant upon the image of a person not present? For the association psychologist this question does not exist at all. For he does not notice that here the direct imagination was for an instant overcome by the antagonistic inverse imagination, since the word "association" covers over for him the processes of the direct along with the inverse imagination as well as their antagonistic relation to one another. It is as though a physicist did not notice that the ray of light which passes through a prism has penetrated from the medium of air into the medium of glass and back again into the medium of air and has thereby twice undergone a change of direction, a refraction. Figuratively we may perhaps also speak of a refraction of our fantasy, and indeed likewise of a twofold refraction, for our attention was diverted from the present person A to the absent person B and directed back again to the first. The association psychologist, however, notices of this wonderful process only so much, that the person B resembles the person A, and draws from this the peculiar conclusion that it must be the similarity which effected the whole process. As though "similarity" were a power capable of extinguishing as it were what is present and of conjuring for an instant something not present before our mind. May one personify the concepts which the human mind forms for itself and raise them to efficacious powers? Similarity is after all only a concept which the human mind gains through comparison of objects, but no personality, no efficacious mind, no force or energy of nature which could free us from the bonds of the present and lead us over to something not present. And the same holds also of the remaining so-called "laws of association" of which Hume, James Mill, John Stuart Mill, and so forth have dreamed. If one considers, for example, the Humean laws of association, one recognizes at first glance, as Wundt too sets forth with fine irony, that they represent a mere playing with categories of concepts. "Just as," says Wundt, "fire, water, air, and earth are categories under which at a naive standpoint of consideration one may if need be accommodate the appearances of nature, just so would associations according to similarity and contrast, according to simultaneity and succession represent convenient logical templates for the transition from one representation to another. If then the representations X and Y are associated with one another, one will be able to say at one time that they were associated through similarity or, if this does not apply, that they were associated through contrast. If these templates do not fit well, then the association will let itself be brought about by the fact that X and Y were observed together simultaneously, and if this too does not fit, then temporal succession can render the most excellent service. In short, the associated representations X and Y will after all always stand in some logical relation to one another, hence this relation will be able to be brought under some logical category, so that one can then say that this category effected the association of the representations." And this theory was devised not by scholastic philosophers of the Middle Ages but by modern thinkers who are regarded as the heads of the modern "empiricist" tendency! That power, now, through which our mind is quite involuntarily led over from what is present to what is not present and led back again to what is present, reveals itself to everyone who really lets himself be instructed by experience in a wholly unforced manner. That power is namely the human temperament with its never-resting surges of feeling, mood, and passion. When some unsatisfied physical or mental need awakens in us and announces itself through ever more loudly throbbing feelings or emotions, then, as is well known, it is all over with the calm lingering upon the sight of what is present. And then fantasy strays off to images of the past and the future or in general of what is not. In themselves our drives and the feelings and emotions awakened by them are indeed blind, but inasmuch as they combine with processes of imagination they become as it were seeing and purposive. It is one of the greatest riddles of our life-process how feelings can combine with imaginings and fashion a world of fantasy which is able to free our mind from the sensible fetters of the environment. For association psychology, to be sure, this fundamental problem of our life of temperament is not present at all. For it does not notice that between the representation X which belongs to the environment and the representation Y associated with it which does not belong to the environment a tone of feeling must step as mediator, since otherwise the involuntary transition from what is present to what is not present would be impossible. For all our sensations are accentuated by feeling, and this accentuation of feeling, which in the face of the same appearance can in general be a very different one in different persons, is really what is able to lead our consciousness over to something not present. If I see, for example, the person A, then a feature X in his face may be so accentuated by feeling in me that I am involuntarily reminded of the person B. Someone else, who sees the person A and also knows the person B quite well, will nevertheless not be reminded of B at the sight of A, because in him the feature X is not accentuated by feeling in such a manner as in me. It is of importance to remark that the transition from the direct into the inverse imagination of which there is talk here happens in a wholly involuntary manner. Involuntarily the imagination glides along the lines of the things which are found in our environment, and just as involuntarily it glides off, under the influence of feelings and emotions, to shapes and images which belong to the inverse imagination. But we are able—and upon this rests the human character of our intelligence—to steer our processes of imagination up to a certain degree voluntarily: we can namely compel our imagination not to stray to absent things but to persist with the environment, so that we may gain as exact an intuition as possible of what is present. A perfect chaining of our fantasy to the real environment can of course never succeed for us, because the imagination, in consequence of the accentuation by feeling of what is sensed, is quite involuntarily drawn over—even if only for brief instants—into the realm of what is not present. Conversely, however, we can deliberately draw the imagination away from what is present and turn it toward what is not present, as is always the case when we wish to give ourselves over to our thoughts. A perfect sinking into thoughts will of course not be possible, because the impressions of the environment lead our imagination back again and again for instants to what is present. Thus our consciousness during the course of its waking state oscillates continually between what is present and what is not present, and this oscillation will now take place in involuntary fashion, now be deliberately brought about by understanding and will. But whether we dwell with our imagination upon what is present or upon what is not present, whether we let it hold sway involuntarily or steer it voluntarily: always the shapes which it brings before us will have to be grasped through virtual movements. It is the same virtual movements through which I perceive the shapes of real things and through which I set arbitrary figures before myself in the inverse imagination. If, for example, I perceive a triangle upon a real pyramid-shaped body standing before me, then this happens just as much by means of imagined movements as when in the inverse imagination I think a triangle drawn before me in the air. This fact, now, is of the highest epistemological significance. For since it is the same imagined movements by means of which I perceive any shapes really present upon bodies and through which I draw fictive figures before myself, I shall be able in the investigation of shapes to hold just as well to my fictive figures as to the forms of real things. Upon this rests the peculiar scientific character of geometry and of pure kinematics. Geometry is the science of space and of spatial structures; but since real space and real spatial structures can be grasped, just as fictive space and fictive spatial structures, only with the help of imagination, and namely of imagined movement, geometry will be no such empirical science as physics or chemistry. An imagined flash of lightning does not, as is well known, have the same properties as a real flash of lightning; hence the physicist will not be able to say that the investigation of real appearances can be replaced by that of imagined processes. But the space of perception has exactly the same properties as the space of the inverse imagination, and so the geometer will rightly assert that the exploration of the properties of real spatial structures can be carried out in the inverse imagination. This is indeed the wonder of space, that in real perception it shows no properties whatever other than in the inverse imagination, because precisely real space too can be perceived only by means of imagination or of imagined movement. But I designate such a mode of knowledge, for which it is indifferent whether its objects of investigation are given in reality or in imagination, as neutral. In this sense geometry is a neutral science, because the propositions which it finds in the inverse imagination must at once have validity also for direct perception, and because it consists in general of nothing but such propositions for whose validity it is indifferent whether they are referred to the world of reality or to that of the inverse imagination. The same holds for pure kinematics or phoronomy, which, considering movement merely as change of place, abstracts from the mass of what is movable and from its mechanical effects. For if one abstracts from the effects of movement and considers it merely from the point of view that it is a change of place of spatial structures in the course of an interval of time, then all the properties of this change of place can be studied upon imagined movement just as well as upon real movement, because after all real movement too cannot be grasped otherwise than through the imagined. In order therefore to establish the peculiar scientific character of geometry and phoronomy, it is by no means necessary to take refuge in Kant's distinction between knowledge a priori and a posteriori. It suffices to discern that the perception of the real world rests not only upon sensation but also upon imagination, and that in particular real movement and the relations of space can be perceived only by means of imagination. Geometry and phoronomy are sciences which have human imagination or virtual movement as their foundation, so that if our imagination were otherwise constituted, our intuition of space and of movement too would have to be otherwise constituted. The axioms of geometry are propositions by which the nature of our imagination is characterized just as much as the physical-chemical appearances which we perceive in space. The proposition that through two points only one straight line can pass, that further the straight line is the shortest path between two points, and that through a given point only one parallel to a straight line can be drawn, as well as the remaining geometrical axioms, are of fundamental significance not only for the real world but in just the same measure for the nature of our imagination. To an imagination other than ours there would also correspond another geometry and kinematics than ours. The new geometries which the great mathematicians Bolyai and Lobachevsky devised, and which one is wont to designate as non-Euclidean geometry in contrast to Euclidean geometry, which refers to our real world, may be regarded as attempts to break through the limits of human imagination and have therefore a quite incomparable epistemological significance. Unfortunately this significance has been clarified neither by the mathematicians nor by the philosophers of our days, so that the non-Euclidean and multidimensional geometries wrought a great devastation even in the best heads and seduced them to the most questionable sins against the fundamental principles of logic, the exposition of which I reserve for another occasion. But the first step which we take toward the correct philosophical appreciation of non-Euclidean and multidimensional geometry is the recognition that it is imagination, the virtual movement, to which we owe the perception of real movements and relations of space. After we have in such wise sufficiently emphasized the significance of virtual movement, we must now raise the question: what is virtual movement? To what kind of processes does it belong? First of all it is to be remarked that it is by no means a mental or psychic activity, of which one convinces oneself through the following consideration. If one pronounces the word triangle with understanding, then one notices with some attention that one has quite involuntarily—and to be sure also with great fleetingness—at once also drawn a concrete triangular figure before oneself in fantasy. Similarly does it stand with the understanding pronunciation of words such as ocean, cliff, waterfall, and so forth, indeed with the whole of human speech: we accompany our text of words and thoughts as it were with illustrations which are produced now by lively, now by stunted virtual movements. But no level-headed thinker will exchange his text of thoughts for the illustrations which his more or less excited fantasy adds. For the sense of the word "triangle" or of any other word is a general one: the word is employed for the designation of all specimens of a genus, whereas the illustration which is added by our imagination to the word triangle or to any other word is always something individual, concrete. The triangle which I set before myself through an imagined movement cannot be at the same time an acute-angled, a right-angled, and an obtuse-angled one; rather, the sense of the word "triangle" comprehends all the triangles that are possible. In short, the imagined movement, as well as real movement, always produces only a single figure. Words, on the contrary, have a sense only because, and are symbols of concepts only because, they can be referred to arbitrarily many individual things belonging to the same species. But although it costs only a slight logical exertion to distinguish our text of words and thoughts from the accompanying and, as has been said, often most rudimentary illustrations of fantasy, there is nevertheless a philosophical school which consistently and in principle confuses the thought with the virtual movement illustrating it, or, more correctly speaking, denies the existence of the thought and puts in its place the concrete individual image produced through virtual movement. The most energetic chief representative of this English-sensualistic-nominalistic school is Berkeley. He boldly declares that all our ideas are individual ideas, that there are no abstract ideas at all, and he proves the proposition roughly in the following manner: let one attempt to form the idea of a triangle which is at once equilateral and scalene and isosceles, and one will convince oneself that this involves an impossibility. But the abstract idea of a triangle would require that we form the idea of a triangle which unites within itself those incompatible properties: hence there is no abstract idea of a triangle or of any other things. Evidently he confuses the idea of a triangle with that illustration which we produce by means of imagination in order to exemplify the abstract idea in concrete fashion. We illustrate, as has been said, all our ideas through virtual drawings, but we must never confuse these illustrations with the ideas themselves. If, for example, a man born blind represents a triangle to himself, then he illustrates the idea of the triangle in quite another manner than we who see, for he is able to illustrate the triangle to himself only through the fantasy of touch, while we do it also with the fantasy of sight. Nevertheless the abstract idea of a triangle remains the same for the man born blind as for the seeing man. One and the same idea can be illustrated in the most various ways without therefore forfeiting its ideality. We whites illustrate the word "man" in our imagination quite involuntarily with the silhouette of a white; the Japanese, on the contrary, will for this purpose just as involuntarily produce the silhouette of a Mongol: nevertheless both have the same abstract idea of man. Someone can, when he thinks of his friend Hans, behold him in imagination sitting or standing, weeping or also laughing, and so forth, and nevertheless know that he always has the one "Hans" in mind; for if he took every illustration which his imagination produces of "Hans" for another person, then it would be all up with his understanding. Whoever therefore denies abstract ideas denies the understanding: Berkeley too concedes the possibility of abstract ideas, but because he is unable to make clear to himself the difference between his abstract concept and its concrete illustration in fantasy, and because he is further filled with a great aversion to abstractions, it gives him joy to dispute them out of the world. Incomprehensible only is why anyone philosophizes, and moreover philosophizes in so abstract a manner as Berkeley, if he is so mortally hostile to abstractions that he would most gladly exterminate them. How little imagined movement is anything psychic comes forth in vivid fashion when one fixes one's eye sharply upon its role within the human life-process. The infant is able in the earliest epoch of its life, as we have already remarked, only to travel aimlessly to and fro with its little hands; were it never to learn to steer the gaze and to lead the limbs toward a goal, then it would also never come to an activity of perception, for whoever is never master of his movements can also never find anything and transpose nothing sensed to any place, and hence is also capable of no perception, because perception becomes possible only where a place is assigned to what is sensed, so that it finds its ranging within the general spatial connection. But how does there arise out of the chance eye movements of the infant a steerable and then also a self-steering gaze? And how does there arise out of the shapeless traveling to and fro of the hands a gradually forming, purposive movement? Not otherwise than through the help of the developing imagined movement, which prescribes to real movement paths at first uncertain, then ever more determinately formed. Only when after the elapse of three to four months the virtual and the real movements of the gaze and of the limbs are sufficiently strengthened can there be talk also of an activity of perception in the infant. From this it is to be seen that imagined movement is a temporally preceding condition of the activity of perception and not this activity itself, and hence can also have no psychic character. Processes of perception are acts of consciousness; imagined movements, however, are no psychic activities, for long before intelligence begins to stir, the aimless movements of gaze and hand of the child must be ground into formed movements through the virtual movements. But the nonpsychic character of imagined movements comes forth still more clearly in the grown man. When we must make a still unaccustomed movement in the handling of an instrument, we easily become aware of the imagined movement which is to show the real one the way; but the more we have practiced the movement, the more imperceptible becomes its virtual steering, and the more it acquires the character of a so-called "automatized movement." What is meant by this expression psychology gives us as good as no information about. And yet it is here a matter of one of the most interesting and significant problems of the science of vital processes. Someone can, sunk in thought and paying no heed whatever to his steps, take a walk, and he does not wonder afterward at all that his legs functioned to good purpose without having been held to it by their commander, by consciousness. One says the legs did their duty "automatically," and believes the matter is disposed of with this word. But what does one mean by this enigmatic word? Does one believe perhaps that the automatically moving legs are robbed of their signpost, of imagined movement? Were the automatic movements to have lost their helmsman, virtual movement, then they would have to sink back to that primitive stage of development which manifests itself in the infant through the aimlessly traveling little hands. An automatized movement remains a purposive movement only because it has not lost its helmsman, namely virtual movement; to be sure, this virtual movement has forfeited contact with consciousness. It is what has been automatized, that is, switched out of connection with consciousness. By an automatized movement we therefore understand such a movement whose virtual rudder has been switched out of connection with consciousness, that is, automatized. The more we practice a voluntary movement, with the smaller an expenditure of conscious imagined movement can it be steered, and if the required minimum of expenditure is reached, then the imagined movement can, temporarily loosening its connection with the activity of consciousness, become as it were independent and go on working without the supervision of consciousness, whereby consciousness experiences a certain relief and can turn its attention to other activities. The chief result of this consideration is the important insight that there is an "automatic imagination" which steers our movements even when it no longer stands under the supervision of consciousness. And this insight signifies the first step in the scientific theory of the voluntary movements of man and of animals. At the same time it comes clearly forth that imagined movements are in themselves nothing psychic. They can indeed stand under the immediate command of consciousness, but they are able also temporarily to detach themselves from it in order to go on working in the most purposive manner. Most impressively do this detachment from consciousness and the nonpsychic character of imagined movement come to light in those peculiar states in which the activity of consciousness of a person appears paralyzed and yet the processes of sensation of the senses and of imagination continue to subsist, as is the case in hypnosis. This is in its essence a specific paralysis of the brain through which consciousness is switched off in such a way that the pathways leading to it nevertheless remain open, so that processes of sensation can take their course undisturbed. The skin can remain receptive to processes of tactile, the half-closed eye to processes of light, the ear to processes of tonal sensation; all these sensory processes are able to stimulate virtual movements which are translatable into real movements, without consciousness having the slightest share in all these processes. The hypnotized person is in such wise a sensing, feeling, fantasying automaton, that is, the processes of sensation, of feeling, and of fantasy which play themselves out in him combine with no activity of consciousness, but are steered by the consciousness of a second person, that of the hypnotist. He therefore presents as it were a distorted image of a thinking being, for he seems to sense, to feel, and to have imagination like a conscious being, while nevertheless consciousness is wanting to him. Whoever cannot distinguish the activity of consciousness from the processes of sensation, of feeling, and of imagination, let him once attend hypnotic experiments and observe attentively the hypnotist and his medium: for here he will find distributed over two persons what he was unable to separate from one another in one person. He will convince himself in vivid fashion that in the medium processes of sensation, of feeling, and of imagination surge to which not a trace of consciousness adheres, and that in the hypnotist there is to be found the corresponding complementary activity of consciousness which is missed in the medium. Whoever now is clear that sensations, feelings, and imaginings are nothing psychic will not be all too greatly surprised by such a paralysis of the brain in which the said three processes can continue while every activity of consciousness is switched off. The proper significance of hypnotic experiments is to be sought in the first rank in this, that they cast a new light upon the healthy mental activity of man. These experiments show what a world-wide difference subsists between mere nervous life and a steering of nervous life through one's own consciousness. In the hypnotized person nervous life remains just as excitable from without as in the waking man: outer impressions awaken in him intense processes of sensation, mighty upheavals of feeling, and stimulate also the passive play of lively phantasms, so that the processes of imagined movement can also lead over to corresponding real movements. Nevertheless in all these processes of a highly developed nervous life nothing is to be discovered of a knowing and steering activity of consciousness. If, then, there is anywhere an opportunity for the experimenting investigator of nature to grasp the infinite difference between autonomous mental activity and the mere processes of nervous life, this is surely the case in the interesting domain of hypnotic experiment. Unfortunately, however, hypnotism has not been exploited precisely in this most significant aspect. There prevails in modern psychology and physiology a boundless confusion of the concepts of the activity of consciousness and of the nervous-vital process: one intermingles the mental with the nervous-living in a quite unpardonable manner, although precisely hypnotism offers vast sums of experimental facts which seem made for the clarification of these fundamental concepts. One convinces oneself upon the occasion of every hypnotic session in a most vivid manner that mental activity is something essentially other than a mere vital stream of processes of sensation, of feeling, and of imagination. Sensations, feelings, and imaginings are, considered in themselves, purely "vital processes," which can indeed come to consciousness or be steered by consciousness, without however being in themselves activity of consciousness. Originally no level-headed man is inclined to count pains or the excitations of hunger, thirst, voluptuousness, and so forth among the psychic activities, and for this reason one distinguishes the "sensible nature" of man from his "mental" one. This is of course an uncertain distinction which in this form is not correct and must be replaced by the conceptual separation of the "vital process" and the "activity of consciousness": but it could very well have become the point of departure of a genuinely monistic philosophy which brings the vital and the conscious in human nature equally into their own. Descartes, who, stiffening himself upon his proud "I think" [cogito], set forth the sensible nature of man as the source of errors and of evil, conjured up through the rationalistic one-sidedness of his otherwise exalted and rigorous mode of thought a counterrevolution which was to become in a high degree fateful for the development of the new psychology. For English science, whose ideal is vitality, took up the cause of the suppressed "sensible nature" of man in such a manner that it raised the "sensible," or better the vital, to the psychic and set forth the sensation of the senses downright as "idea," as an elementary idea which was to lie at the ground of man's remaining ideas. This confusion of the vital with the mental, or more correctly this resolving of everything mental into vitality through an ambiguous "psychology" which contains neither a doctrine of life nor a doctrine of mind, had as its consequence that for more than two centuries biology and the science of mind have suffered from a chronic confusion of their fundamental concepts. And it seems that this confusion reaches its high point precisely in our days, which comes to expression in the fact that "psychologistics" threatens to devour all philosophical thinking and carries the germs of its Babylonian confusion of concepts not only into the sciences of mind but also into the natural sciences. Possibly, however, we already stand before a turn of modern thinking which will lead to the recovery of the modern mind; at least the signs multiply that a clear conceptual separation of the vital and the mental is being ever more earnestly striven after also in the circle of the "physiological psychologists." For the most advanced investigators of this great school declare that psychology must constitute itself as a branch of the natural sciences, which has its complete justification and is joyfully to be welcomed insofar as that "psychology" which is pursued experimentally is according to its whole essence in fact throughout only vitalistic natural science, which at present still suffers from a mingling of the fundamental concepts of the mental and the vital, but which can be freed from this hereditary evil through a rigorous critical-epistemological purification. But still more promising than the pressing of the physiological psychologists toward a clarified natural science is the mighty striving of the "neovitalists" after a reform of biology, which is to furnish once and for all the rigorous proof that the living can never be reduced to something mechanical, that it is therefore impossible ever to resolve biology into physics and chemistry. To be sure, this highly significant tendency in modern natural science still suffers from many an epistemological perversity or mystical vagueness: but at bottom it nevertheless designates the way of that progress which will lead to the distinction of a vitalistic and a mechanistic method in biology. We can take the riddle of life scientifically in hand—as I seek to show in another place—from two directions, namely through direct mechanistic (physical-chemical) research and further through indirect vitalistic methods of measurement; these can lay claim to exactitude just as much as the former. There will therefore have to be formed alongside "mechanistic biology," and in the most intimate union with it, a "vitalistic biology." The theory of fantasy which I here furnish would be a philosophical contribution to this vitalistically minded biology. In my conception, namely, vitalism is an unconditionally victorious doctrine because in reality there are vital facts as distinct from mechanical (physical-chemical) ones. Sensations, feelings, and imaginings form three classes of vital facts which are characterized by this, that they everywhere hang together only with a consciousness, or, as one may express it, can be immediately attested only through a consciousness. Mechanical facts, on the contrary, can have arbitrarily many witnesses or observers. If, for example, two persons see one and the same flash of lightning, then this flash of lightning presents a physical fact, whereas the two experiences which two persons have of the same physical fact must be designated as two vital facts. A single physical event can excite in n persons n experiences, hence n vital processes, so that it is for the discerning a logical impossibility to confuse the concept of the mechanical with that of the vital. If now, on the other hand, one wishes to distinguish the vital also from the mental, let one take refuge in a hypnotic experiment during whose course the hypnotist calls out to his medium: "Now a flash of lightning has struck the tree here before our window!" One will see by what intense experiences the hypnotized person shows himself shaken, without there combining with the vital processes suggested to him even the slightest activity of consciousness. We have thus obtained a quite determinate answer to the question to what kind of happenings the processes of imagination belong. To be sure, our processes of fantasy generally combine with activities of consciousness and, through this being-bound-up with the mind, seduce us into holding the processes of imagination themselves to be something mental, but they are in themselves only vital processes, quite as much as our experiences of sensation and of feeling. Imagination plays in the realm of the vital a role similar to that of movement in the mechanical world. One could designate it as the vital analogue, as the vital equivalent, or as the vital image of movement. Just as a body is able to escape from the society of other bodies in mechanical fashion only through movement, so a life-process can free itself from the clamps of its environment only through imagination. It is a wonder without parallel that life, without stirring from the place where it finds itself, can nevertheless comport itself as though it had slipped away to another place in space or to another place in time. This being carried away of the life-process from the spatio-temporal standpoint at which it in reality persists is called the process of fantasy. Mechanically life may show itself banished to one place; in vital respect it is nevertheless not banished to that place, but can within the mechanical fetters yet be free of these fetters. This self-liberation of the life-process from the bonds which in reality hold it captive is called in the popular manner of expression "the flight of fantasy." If, then, there is any process whatever by which life sets itself off with perfect distinctness from the mechanical, it is the process of imagination. From the mechanistic standpoint a body is always just where it is; considered from the vitalistic standpoint, however, a living body can dwell somewhere quite other than where it is held captive in mechanical fashion. Now as life nowhere so distinctly confronts the mechanical as in the process of imagination, so this is to be regarded as the highest unfolding of the vital. Sensation and feeling are indeed likewise vital processes, yet in them life shows itself still wholly fettered to the mechanical location; for in sensation nothing further announces itself than an immediately present physical-chemical process, and in feeling nothing further makes itself known than an immediately present state of a living being; life is thus bound to the present through the sensations and feelings, and only because sensations and feelings also stimulate imagination is life able to roam into the spatio-temporal distance while mechanically it dwells at one location. Thus it now also becomes intelligible why even what is present cannot be perceived without the help of imagination. For what takes place, say, two meters distant from our body can evidently be perceived at this determinate distance only through imagination. An activity of perception is not possible without imagination. Modern philosophy of sensation (modern psychologism) knows no process of imagination, but speaks of a "projection of sensation" into the external world toward that place where the perceived image is found. It shoots sensation as it were out of a pistol toward the place of the perceived thing and believes through such a "theory" to have found a way to the explanation of our perception of space. Now, however, the word "projection of sensation" says just as little as the word "local sign." All these technical expressions, such as for example also the term "association," are there only to cover over that process of imagined movement through which the perception of a spatially ordered world of appearance is first made possible. Modern philosophy of sensation thus sins not only against the human mind, in that it resolves psychic activity into the mere vital process, but transgresses still far more against this vital process, in that it eradicates a highest component, the process of imagination, and thereby makes the formation of a natural-scientific theory of the vital substratum of all activity of perception an impossibility. Whoever supposes that a perception is possible on the ground of sensations could equally assert that our whole spatial world of appearance is only one-dimensional. As little as a sensible world of appearance can be forced into the course of a geometrical line, so little is it possible to perceive a sensible world with the mere function of sensation without the help of the two other functions of nervous life, namely of feeling and of fantasy. In a complete doctrine of fantasy the following problems will therefore have to be treated: a) the relation of the processes of imagination to the acts of consciousness, whereby the significance of fantasy for all logical, aesthetic, and ethical activities of the human mind is laid open; b) the relation of the processes of imagination to the processes of sensation and of feeling as well as the relation of these three components to the vegetative ground of life. By the vegetative ground of life is here meant the proper life of the cells, tissues, and organs of the organism, that deeply veiled proper life of all the constituents of our body which in its immediacy can never come to consciousness and of whose presence we have mediate tidings only through the changing feelings which rise up to consciousness out of the unfathomable depth of the ground of life; c) the relation of fantasy or of imagined movement to real or mechanical movement, whereby one of the most important problems of modern natural philosophy, namely the relation of vitalism to mechanism, is unrolled. Of these problems we can here only touch upon the first and the third, since we should otherwise have to develop at once the fundamental features of a philosophy of mind as well as a natural philosophy. Here only the relation of the processes of imagination to the processes of sensation and of feeling interests us, that is, we seek to ground a vitalistic theory of imagination. We saw that it is the processes of sensation which stimulate our direct imagination, but it must now be emphasized that the process of imagination thus awakened also acts back stimulatingly upon sensation. If, for example, a foreign body touches our body at the point P, then the sensation of contact stimulates in us the process of imagined movement through which we point to the point P where the contact took place. But inasmuch as we aim with the imagined movement at a point of our body, there is in this point in turn excited a sensation which, to be sure, on account of its very slight intensity usually does not come to consciousness. It is, however, well known that when someone sharply observes a point of our body, we feel a sensation at that point—not, say, because from the foreign eye an exciting fluid streams toward the fixed point, but because our own imagination begins to occupy itself with that fixed point, and the imagined movement which aims at a point of our life awakens a sensation there as well. It belongs among our most everyday experiences that every movement which we perform in imagination is able to awaken the most manifold sensations. If, for example, one stretches out the hand in imagination toward the metal door handle, then there is released in the hand not only a sensation of contact but also a sensation of cold as upon the real grasping of the metal. To be sure, these induced sensations are of very slight intensity, but there are cases where this intensity can also attain a higher degree. If one carries, say, in imagination a slice of lemon into the mouth—which usually happens when someone does this before our eyes—then this imagined movement can call forth so lively a sensation of the taste of citric acid that the induced sensation seems in intensity almost to equal the real one. In short, it is the first chief law of our direct imagination that, when it is stimulated by any sensation E, it for its part induces the essentially identical sensation E₁. Were a sensation not to stimulate the process of imagination and the process of imagination for its part not to be accompanied by the corresponding sensation, then it would never come to an activity of perception. If a perception is to come about, then the organism must react to the received process of sensation, and only this reaction to the sensation leads to an act of perception. But we react to sensations with virtual movements, which for their part induce sensations in us. The most interesting thing in this state of affairs is first of all that imagination is not only a consequent process of sensation but for its part produces sensations, or, as one expresses it more aptly: induces them. One commonly supposes that sensations are stimulated only through some agent of the external world; one should not, however, overlook that the imagined movement is a sensation-awakening process, and that the sensation induced by it is just as much a real sensation as the sensation which is called forth by an external agent. When I say that a sensation must be thrown back into itself in order that it may lead to an act of perception, then nothing mystical is thereby meant, but only that the sensation must induce an imagined movement and this an identical sensation in order that a perception may come about. One must therefore say that the sensory nervous process stimulates a second nervous process, that of virtual movement, and leads this back to the first, so that the nerve pathways of the whole process form a line closed in upon itself. After we have illuminated—if only fleetingly—the relation of fantasy to sensation, we must now fix our eye upon its intimate relation to the life of feeling, because thereby a new light will fall upon the inverse imagination. Life of sensation and life of feeling are so closely bound up with one another that it is most difficult to separate the two conceptually from one another, and that it is hard to succeed in setting forth the relation of fantasy to the one or to the other. Every sensation is also accompanied by a tone of feeling peculiar to it, and conversely every feeling is accompanied by tones of sensation. That this must be so is easy to comprehend. An agent, such as for example a weight pressing upon the skin, stimulates not only a process of sensation but also irritates the proper life of the cells and tissues upon which it presses, and brings about such alterations in their conditions of nourishment, hence in the circulation of the blood, that these alterations of the vegetative life must make themselves known to consciousness through more or less lively processes of feeling. On the other hand we are wont to characterize our feelings of pain with expressions borrowed from the language of sensation, and we speak accordingly of stabbing, throbbing, boring, burning, and so forth pains, as a sign that in our processes of feeling sensations too make themselves noticeable. But although sensations and feelings everywhere enter into intimate fusions with one another, their conceptual distinction is nevertheless of high importance in principle, because ultimately in feeling only the transformation within one's own life-process ever comes to word, whereas sensation always points to physical-chemical processes foreign to life. Between feeling and sensation there subsists then a polar difference analogous to that between the living and the nonliving. Only the nonliving is sensed, only life is felt. But because the vital process is everywhere accompanied by physical-chemical processes within the organism, feeling too will have to be accompanied by tones of sensation and conversely sensation by tones of feeling. The polar difference of sensation and feeling makes itself known also in the whole structure of the nervous system, for this can in a certain sense be designated as a double system, insofar as the processes of sensation are evidently bound predominantly to the brain and spinal cord, the processes of feeling on the contrary to the sympathetic system. When therefore we say that sensations fuse with tones of feeling and feelings with tones of sensation, this has, translated into the physiological, the sense that the excitation of certain sensory nerve pathways sets sympathetic nerve pathways into co-excitation, and that conversely the excitation proceeding from sympathetic fibrils can bring the sensory fibrils into co-vibration. In short, one will be permitted to speak of sensory and sympathetic nerve pathways coordinated with one another. There lies expressed herein a law of the reciprocal coordination of the life of sensation and the life of temperament, of which of course sensualistic psychology knows nothing to say. After these considerations concerning the connection—still shrouded in deep veils—of our life of sensation and of feeling, it will also be possible for us to set forth the second chief law of fantasy, which refers to its refraction or deflection. If some agent excites a sensation in us, then in the sense of the law just set forth a tone of feeling too will sound along in the sympathetic nervous system. But it very easily happens that besides the normally coordinated tone of feeling still other related diffuse tones of feeling sound along, which has as its consequence that the process of imagination which is awakened through the sensation undergoes a deflection. Only so long as a sensation awakens the tone of feeling exactly coordinated with it will imagination return to the present sensation, mirror it in the sense explained above, and linger with it. But in the same instant in which another tone of feeling associates itself with the appertaining one, imagination will be seized by this and suffer a deflection. It is this fantasy deflected from the present sensation that we designate as inverse fantasy, and to which we owe it that our life-process, and in connection with it our consciousness, is able to dwell upon something not present. The process of sensation need only become stronger or weaker, awaken a more or less pleasurable feeling, bring about a greater tension or relaxation of temperament, in order that a change of the tone of feeling may seize imagination and deflect it from the present impression. The deflected imagination will now for its part induce a sensation, and indeed of something not present. But how is this possible? Quite simply through this, that between the sensations and the feelings a law of coordination holds sway, as we set forth above: a law according to which sympathetic pathways are coordinated with the sensory nerve pathways. Let us assume that a process of sensation running its course in the sensory pathway A stimulates a process of feeling not only in the corresponding sympathetic pathway a but also in a neighboring pathway b; then imagination will thereby be deflected, and indeed toward that sensation B which is coordinated with the tone of feeling b. If, then, it once stands thus, that the sensations are coordinated with corresponding feelings, then a new feeling will lead our imagination over to the new sensation corresponding to it, that is, imagination will induce a sensation of something not present. The second law of fantasy therefore runs: our imagination is deflected from our sensations through the alteration of the tones of feeling which accompany them, and indeed it will induce such sensations as are coordinated with the altered tones of feeling. One can metaphorically designate this law as the law of refraction of fantasy. There is no finer example of this law than the dream life of man, which is shrouded in such deep darkness only because the nature of human fantasy was never made the object of a positive research. In sleep our life of sensation is, as is well known, the more lowered the deeper the sleep is. Brain and spinal cord, these commanders of our animal life, are as it were dethroned, and the vegetative life and its regulator, the sympathetic nervous system, take over the dominion in the whole domain of our vital processes. Sleep is really nothing further than a necessary periodic recoil of our vegetative life against the encroachments of the animal life-process in the waking state. The waking labor of life, which stands in the service of the organism, impairs without interruption the proper life of the myriads of cells out of which the organism is built up. How much this is the case emerges, for example, also from the fact that we can protect ourselves from fatigue through skillful alternation in the waking labor of life and preserve our freshness of performance up to a certain degree. The economy of our organization holds out, however, only until the reaction-period of sleep sets in. Under the dominion of the sympathetic nervous system regeneration then takes place. But if the sympathetic nervous system was seized during the waking labor by more intense agitations of temperament, or if in sleep a strong vegetative labor, for instance the labor of digestion, is still being performed, then the excitation of the sympathetic nervous system continues to sound on to such a degree that thereby the processes of imagination too are drawn into sympathy. Dream life begins. The dream is the typical presentation of a purely inverse process of fantasy, that is, of a process of imagination which is stimulated by feeling. If the direct imagination is rooted in the life of sensation, the inverse grows up out of the life of feeling. In the waking state, however, the inverse fantasy never comes to complete dominion, because it is hindered by the lively life of sensation. The waking state is to be conceived as an oscillation between the processes of the direct and the inverse imagination. The manner and way of this oscillation, but quite particularly the magnitude of its elongation, is the gauge of talent and of genius. The more strongly the direct imagination is able to chain itself to what is present and to be absorbed in it, and with the greater force the liberation from its bonds and the sinking into the inverse imagination takes place—the mightier, therefore, the wave-beat of life between the two shores of the present and the not-present—the more is such a life-process called to be the bearer of a great talent. Only it must not thereby be forgotten that this "wave-beat" is a wholly involuntary one and that there is also still a conscious dominion of the mind over the alternation between direct and inverse imagination. It is only the steering power of consciousness over the direct and inverse imagination that constitutes genius. Whereas in waking the temporal phases of the direct and the inverse imagination alternate with one another in some highly variable rhythms, in dream there can be no talk of such an alternation, because the life of sensation is as good as switched off and the process of imagination is stimulated only through the sympathetic nervous system. Now, between a merely imagined and a real movement a distinction can be made only if consciousness possesses the choice between direct and inverse imagination. This is what the dreamer lacks, which has as its consequence that he holds what is imagined to be what is real. The psychology of the dream thereby becomes intelligible. The images which the dream brings before us are distinguished by their extraordinary liveliness; the images of the waking inverse fantasy, on the contrary, are often rather pale and uncertain. For in the waking state the inverse fantasy must seek to assert itself over against its mighty opponent, the direct fantasy, which can always happen only intermittently and for a few instants, because the real environment at once again lays the processes of imagination in its chains; in sleep, on the contrary, the direct imagination is overcome, so that the inverse process of imagination can unhindered induce the most gorgeously colored sensations. At bottom, however, there is according to their whole essence no difference at all between the images of the waking inverse fantasy and the images of the dream; the former can therefore with full right be designated as waking dream images. For in the arising of a dream image two processes cooperate: firstly, tones of feeling stimulate the imagined movement, and secondly, this induces the sensations coordinated with the tones of feeling. But both marks fit perfectly also the images of the waking inverse fantasy, so that, apart from an accidental difference of intensity, no difference at all may be made between them. To prevent misunderstandings, let the concept of "induced sensation" be considered more closely. When in imagination we stretch out the hand toward something, this imagined movement will induce a sensation as though we had really touched the object of our longing. In the waking state, however, the induced sensation is too weak, so that it usually is not noticed at all. But if the dreamer in the dream imagination stretches out the hand toward the object of his longing, then this imagined movement will induce so intense a sensation of the contact with that object that it equals in intensity a direct sensation in the waking state. In themselves, however, induced sensations are processes of exactly the same nature as those awakened through an external mechanical action upon our organism. Let one therefore not be all too greatly astonished if the vital process of imagination too can induce a process of sensation. Induced sensations are just as real processes resting upon a nervous process as the sensations called forth through an external action. Modern philosophy of sensation confuses the process of imagination with the induced sensation and thereby annihilates the concept of imagination. It is not the induced sensation but the virtual movement that constitutes the essence of the vital processes of fantasy. To the sensation of red in dream there corresponds the same vital process as to the waking sensation of red; only the manner and way of its stimulation is a different one. The same holds also of the sensation of red which appears in a waking image of fantasy; here this sensation of red will usually be of lesser intensity, which however alters nothing in its proper essence. English theory of sensation in particular is wont also to designate the induced sensations as "reproduced" or as "revived sensations." And it combines with these expressions the quite naive representation that a sensation which has once taken place does not entirely vanish but leaves behind a "trace" in the soul or in the ganglion cell of the nervous system. This "trace" is conceived as a latent or sleeping sensation which can occasionally be awakened out of its twilight state through "association" and thereby becomes a copy of its original essence. But the induced or fantasy sensations are no remnants or copies of impressions that were once already there, as Hume supposes. Every induced sensation is a sensation in which not even the slightest part can be identical with a process of sensation already earlier there and stimulated from without. That sensations can be induced through which our consciousness makes past experiences present to itself rests by no means upon the conserving of former living sensations, but upon that important law which I designated as the coordination of our life of sensation and of feeling, and which physiologically must be expressed as the working together of the cerebrospinal and the sympathetic nervous system. The role of mediator is therein always played by imagination, or more exactly by imagined movement, which in general represents a mediating or connecting vital function and is always in play where something not yet connected is to be brought to consciousness in a connected manner. Nevertheless it happens continually that we are churned through by dark feelings without their being able to become corresponding sensations, and that our consciousness remains standing at unclear intimations without being able to penetrate through to intuition and to knowledge. The more experiences we gather and the more we reflect upon them, the more do sensations and feelings become coordinated with one another and the more easily does it succeed for our feeling, with the help of imagination, to induce a sensation which helps us to intuition and to knowledge. We are grateful to the genuine thinkers and poets because they ease for us the ordering of our inner life, because they enlighten us. From the coordination of the life of sensation and of feeling it by no means follows that the induced sensations of the inverse imagination are any remnants of the externally excited sensations of the direct imagination: but only that the experiences of perception, sensations, and feelings were coordinated and that the inverse fantasy too can make use of them in reverse fashion. With these propositions the foundation is won for a new rational theory of the activity of recollection and memory in man. It would still remain to be set forth in detail how imagination is related to the various kinds of sensation of the senses, the sensations of touch, sight, hearing, and so forth, and how it establishes a connection between them: but however important in principle these questions may be, I must nevertheless deny myself entering upon them within the framework of this small work. It is here for me only a matter of working out in broad strokes the immeasurable significance of what I designate as virtual movement. For this very reason I must in closing still emphasize that imagined movement is a vital process which presents itself as a real nervous process. The vital process of imagination is in the physiological light nothing further than the conflict of two antagonistic reflex movements which reciprocally inhibit one another, so that it can come to no conspicuous visible movement. All impressions which act upon us would necessarily have to discharge themselves in reflex movements, were it not provided for in us, as also in all more highly developed organisms, that many a reflex movement can be inhibited through a counterreflex following upon it exceedingly swiftly. Where this occurs, there we have the feeling of having performed an involuntary movement in imagination. Imagined movement is thus the vital equivalent of two real reflex movements following upon one another in a very short time and therefore canceling one another. But since between the first and second reflex there elapses an interval of time which, however small, is nevertheless at all events measurable, the first will not be able to be entirely canceled, which has as its consequence that every imagined movement must discharge itself in scarcely noticeable real movements. This was already surmised in his day by Carpenter, who with his ideomotor movements touched upon the thoughts here set forth, but arrived at no clarity because he remained caught in a sensualistic psychology.
